</div></section></section><section aria-label="Article content"><section class="body main-article-body"><section class="abstract" id="Abs1"><h2>Abstract</h2>
<p id="Par1">Culture-dependent and -independent studies have provided access to symbiont genes and the functions they play for host sponges. Thus, this work investigates the diversity, presence of genes of pharmacological interest, biological activities and metabolome of the bacteria isolated from the sponges <em>Aplysina caissara</em> and <em>Aplysina fulva</em> collected on the southwestern Atlantic Coast. The genes for Polyketide Synthases types I and II and Nonribosomal Peptide Synthetases were screened in more than 200 bacterial strains obtained, from which around 40% were putatively novel. Twenty-two were positive for at least one of the genes screened. Among them, 12 exhibited antimicrobial activities and one inhibited the proliferation of cancer cells. The metabolic profiles of the 22 strains were analyzed by liquid chromatography with tandem mass spectrometry and molecular network. The Global Natural Products Social Molecular Networking MolNetEnhancer workflow provided a more comprehensive understanding of the metabolic profiles. The results revealed the existence of a wide range of metabolites, however more than half of the compounds could not be identified. It was further observed that the metabolic diversity among the strains varied primarily due to the cultivation medium used. Together the results obtained here revealed the pharmacological potential of the bacteria isolated from <em>Aplysina</em> species.</p>
<section id="sec1"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Supplementary Information</h3>
<p>The online version contains supplementary material available at 10.1007/s42770-024-01588-4.</p></section><section id="kwd-group1" lang="en" class="kwd-group"><p><strong>Keywords:</strong> Sponges, <em>PKS</em>, <em>NRPS</em>, Bioactive compounds, Molecular network, GNPS</p></section></section><section id="Sec1"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Introduction</h2>
<p id="Par2">Marine sponges (phylum Porifera) are sessile and filter-feeding invertebrates considered the oldest metazoan, dating back from over 660 million years ago [<a href="#CR1" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR1">1</a>, <a href="#CR2" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR2">2</a>]. They are known to harbor a large, complex and diverse microbiome, including <em>Bacteria</em>, <em>Archaea</em>, microeukaryotes and virus that can account for roughly 40% of the sponge´s wet weight [<a href="#CR3" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR3">3</a>–<a href="#CR9" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR9">9</a>]. The domain <em>Bacteria</em> is by far the most studied due to its representativeness. Currently more than 25 bacterial phyla are known to be associated with sponges and are enriched in the hosts in comparison with the [<a href="#CR10" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR10">10</a>, <a href="#CR11" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR11">11</a>]. According to the abundance of microbial cells that they harbor, sponges can be classified as high-microbial abundance (HMA), with a bacterial population density of 10⁹ cells/cm³ of sponge tissue, exceeding the concentration found in seawater by 2 to 4 orders of magnitude or low-microbial abundance (LMA), characterized by bacterial densities between 10⁵ to 10⁶ per cm³ of sponge, similar to what is present in seawater [<a href="#CR12" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR12">12</a>, <a href="#CR13" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR13">13</a>].</p>
<p id="Par3">Since the discovery of the sponge bacteriome and their importance in the composition of the host biomass, a question emerged: Is there any mutual and direct benefit for microorganisms and sponges to remain in this interaction? Currently, it is assumed that microorganisms benefit from the supply of nutrients such as nitrogen; protection provided by the hosts and a putative increase in the levels of horizontal gene transfer [<a href="#CR6" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR6">6</a>, <a href="#CR13" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR13">13</a>–<a href="#CR15" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR15">15</a>]. For sponges, the advantages can consist in the provision of food; metabolic waste processing and resistance to infections provoked by other microorganisms [<a href="#CR16" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR16">16</a>–<a href="#CR19" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR19">19</a>]. The bacterial metabolism can also result in the production of several bioactive compounds, which can contribute to the protection of sponges against predators and epibionts [<a href="#CR20" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR20">20</a>, <a href="#CR21" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR21">21</a>]. The most commonly studied chemical classes of secondary metabolites originated from sponge-associated bacteria are terpenes, alkaloids, polyketides and nonribosomal peptides [<a href="#CR14" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR14">14</a>, <a href="#CR16" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR16">16</a>, <a href="#CR22" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR22">22</a>, <a href="#CR23" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR23">23</a>]. These last two classes are also considered important factors for the evolutionary and ecological success of sponges, due to their wide range of potential bioactivities such as antiviral, antiparasitic, antioxidant, antimicrobial, allelopathic, cytotoxic, siderophore and immunosuppressive [<a href="#CR6" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR6">6</a>, <a href="#CR22" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR22">22</a>, <a href="#CR24" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR24">24</a>–<a href="#CR27" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR27">27</a>].</p>
<p id="Par4">Polyketides and nonribosomal peptides can be easily identified by their clusters of biosynthetic genes and their typical multimodular structures when compared to the other classes, characteristic which allowed numerous advances in the field of biotechnology [<a href="#CR13" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR13">13</a>, <a href="#CR22" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR22">22</a>]. The origin of Polyketide Synthases (PKS) and Nonribosomal Peptide Synthetase (NRPS) enzymatic machinery corresponds to these biosynthetic gene clusters. Gene fusion and its aggregated arrangement form an assembly line that can synthesize different products [<a href="#CR28" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR28">28</a>] and is structured in a linear way into modules that have catalytic domains. This multimodular nature facilitates the occurrence of mutations and genetic recombination, which are the driving source of the molecular diversity currently found in both classes [<a href="#CR29" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR29">29</a>, <a href="#CR30" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR30">30</a>].</p>
<p id="Par5">Due to the great variability of the abovementioned structures, polyketide synthases are classified into three types: I, II and III PKS. The type I PKS are large molecules commonly found in bacteria and eukaryotes [<a href="#CR25" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR25">25</a>, <a href="#CR31" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR31">31</a>, <a href="#CR32" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR32">32</a>]. Type II PKS are mostly aromatic structures also present in bacteria [<a href="#CR31" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR31">31</a>, <a href="#CR33" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR33">33</a>, <a href="#CR34" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR34">34</a>]. Meanwhile, type III PKS have simpler enzymatic machinery when compared to the other two and are predominantly found in bacteria and plants [<a href="#CR31" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR31">31</a>, <a href="#CR32" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR32">32</a>]. Although NRPS complex and the type I PKS belong to different protein families, both use similar biosynthetic strategies to form a product [<a href="#CR22" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR22">22</a>, <a href="#CR32" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR32">32</a>, <a href="#CR35" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR35">35</a>, <a href="#CR36" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR36">36</a>]. In addition, NRPS and type I PKS are the most widely tracked genes in studies that explore metagenomes from sponge-associated bacteria. Despite all three types of PKS being present in bacteria, it is estimated that only types I and II are found in marine sponge symbionts [<a href="#CR16" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR16">16</a>, <a href="#CR25" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR25">25</a>, <a href="#CR29" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR29">29</a>].</p>
<p id="Par6">Despite being an area of high biotechnological interest, the investigation of PKS and NRPS complexes from bacterial communities associated with marine sponges is still incipient, especially those found in southwestern Atlantic hosts. Therefore, the present study aims to assess the: (<strong><em>i</em></strong>) diversity and specificity of the culturable bacteria associated with the endemic <em>Aplysina caissara</em> and <em>Aplysina fulva</em> that occur in southwestern Atlantic coast (São Paulo, Brazil), (<strong><em>ii</em></strong>) presence of types I and II PKS as well as NRPS, (<strong><em>iii</em></strong>) biological activities of the bacterial strains isolated from the <em>Aplysina</em> species and (<strong><em>iv</em></strong>) compounds that the culturable bacteriome are capable to produce.</p></section><section id="Sec2"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Materials and methods</h2>
<section id="Sec3"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Sponge species</h3>
<p id="Par7">Individuals of <em>Aplysina caissara</em> Pinheiro and Hajdu, 2001 and <em>Aplysina fulva</em> Pallas, 1766 (Verongiida, Aplysinidae) were collected by scuba in July and October 2018 at Ponta Recife (23°49’30.36”S 45°24’47.879”W) and at the southern rock shores of Praia do Guaecá (23°49’22.8”S 45°28’19.2”W), São Sebastião, north littoral of São Paulo, southwestern Atlantic Ocean (Fig. <a href="#Fig1" class="usa-link">1</a>). In total, 10 sponge samples were collected at depths ranging between 3 and 8 m, placed in sterile plastic bags containing natural seawater, maintained in a cooler and transported to the laboratory for processing. The specimens were rinsed thoroughly with sterile artificial seawater (ASW [<a href="#CR37" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR37">37</a>]) and fragments of each sample were preserved in 70% ethanol for taxonomic identification. The sponge samples were identified by Dr. Gisele Lôbo-Hajdu and Dr. Márcio Custódio using morphological features and photos.
</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="Fig1"><h4 class="obj_head">Fig. 1.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=11885781_42770_2024_1588_Fig1_HTML.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/06eb/11885781/cbec8b4624a7/42770_2024_1588_Fig1_HTML.jpg" loading="lazy" id="MO1" height="457" width="677" alt="Fig. 1"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/Fig1/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Location of sampling sites. <strong>A</strong>. Map of Brazil highlighting the State of São Paulo and <strong>B.</strong> Detailed map of the São Sebastiao Channel area with sampling locations (Ponta do Recife and Praia do Guaeca) marked by red squares</p></figcaption></figure></section></section><section id="Sec4"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Transmission electron microscopy (TEM)</h2>
<p id="Par8">Freshly collected <em>A. caissara</em> and <em>A. fulva</em> sections of 3–5 mm<sup>3</sup> in size were fixed in 2.5% glutaraldehyde in sodium cacodylate buffer 0.2 M for two weeks at 4°C. After, the glutaraldehyde was removed and the samples washed with ASW three times for 50 min each under gentle agitation. Samples were maintained one week in ASW at 4°C, and then postfixed in 2% osmium tetroxide, for 1 h at 4ºC. The pieces were dehydrated in an ethanol series (50%, 70%, 95%, 3 × 99.5%), for 10 min each, and embedded in Epon 812 resin (EMS). Ultra-thin sections were prepared with a microtome EM UC7 (Leica) and contrasted with uranyl acetate and lead citrate. TEM images were acquired in a Jeol 1200 EX (Jeol) at National Center for Structural Biology and Bioimaging from the Federal University of Rio de Janeiro (CENABIO-UFRJ).</p></section><section id="Sec5"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Isolation and identification of bacteria associated with A. fulva and A. caissara</h2>
<p id="Par9">For bacterial cultures, 2.5 g of the choanosome was cut and macerated in 25 mL of calcium/magnesium free artificial seawater [<a href="#CR38" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR38">38</a>] using a sterile mortar and pestle. The supernatant was transferred to a falcon tube, and the remaining sponge residue was discarded. A dilution series was prepared in ASW and 100 µL of dilutions 10<sup>−2</sup> to 10<sup>−6</sup> were plated onto (<strong><em>i</em></strong>) one enriched medium (Brain Heart Infusion, BHI: 37 g.L<sup>−1</sup> and Agar 15 g.L<sup>−1</sup>), (<strong><em>ii</em></strong>) two oligotrophic media (Marine Broth, MB: 37.4 g.L<sup>−1</sup> and Agar 15 g.L<sup>−1</sup>) and Reasoner’s 2 A agar (R2A: 18.2 g.L<sup>−1</sup>) and (<strong><em>iii</em></strong>) two selective media, one for <em>Actinobacteriota</em> (<em>Actinomycete</em> Isolation Agar, AIA: 22 g.L<sup>−1</sup> and glycerol 5 g.L<sup>−1</sup>) and the other for <em>Cyanobacteria</em> (SNAX) prepared as described by [<a href="#CR39" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR39">39</a>]. For the initial cultivation, BHI, R2A and MB were diluted 1:10 to allow the fastidious bacteria to grow and once a colony was pure the media was prepared as suggested by the manufacturer’s instructions. All media were acquired from Difco BD, except agar from Sigma-Aldrich and SNAX, and prepared with ASW, except MB that was prepared with deionized water. Plates were incubated at 21<sup>°</sup>C for seven days and daily checked. Visible colony forming units (CFUs) were collected and purified by three successive streaking on the same medium it was initially obtained.</p>
<p id="Par10">Pure isolated colonies were transferred to the same broth medium that they were obtained (BHI: 37 g.L<sup>−1</sup>, R2A: 18.2 g.L<sup>−1</sup>, MB 37.4 g.L<sup>−1</sup>, the selective media were prepared as explained above without agar) and allowed to grow for 18 h at room temperature with shaking (20–25<sup>°</sup>C, 200 rpm). Two aliquots of each liquid culture were taken and stored in 20% glycerol at −80°C until further use. One milliliter of the remaining culture was centrifuged at 14.000 x <em>g</em> for 2 min, the supernatant was discarded, and the bacterial pellet used for DNA extraction with Wizard Genomic DNA Purification Kit (Promega) according to the manufacturer’s instructions. The quality and quantity of the DNA were checked. For the amplification of the 16S rRNA gene the primer pair 27 F-1492R [<a href="#CR40" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR40">40</a>] was used. The reaction mixture and thermal cycle were performed as described by Nascimento-Silva et al. [<a href="#CR11" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR11">11</a>]. All PCR amplifications were carried out in a C1000 Touch thermal cycler (Bio-Rad). Amplicons with correct gene size (~ 1500 bp) were subjected to sequencing with the chain termination method in an ABI 3500 automatic sequencer (Applied Biosystems) using the forward primer.</p>
<p id="Par11">The generated sequences were manually inspected and trimmed with the Sequence Scanner 2 software (Thermo Fisher Scientific). The 16S rRNA gene sequences were aligned using the SINA web aligner [<a href="#CR41" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR41">41</a>] and classified using the databases present in SILVA [<a href="#CR42" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR42">42</a>, <a href="#CR43" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR43">43</a>] with a confidence of 97%. All 16S rRNA gene sequences were deposited in the National Center for Biotechnology Information (NCBI [<a href="#CR44" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR44">44</a>]), under accession numbers from <a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/nuccore/?term=MZ503279:MZ503487[pacc]" class="usa-link" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">MZ503279 to MZ503487</a> (Table <a href="#MOESM3" class="usa-link">S2</a>).</p>
<p id="Par12">To evaluate if there were bacterial strains exclusively obtained from each <em>Aplysina</em> species, Venn diagram analysis was performed with the online software jvenn (<a href="https://jvenn.toulouse.inrae.fr" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">https://jvenn.toulouse.inrae.fr</a> [<a href="#CR45" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR45">45</a>]), .</p></section><section id="Sec6"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Phylogenetic inferences</h2>
<p id="Par13">The SINA-SILVA software [<a href="#CR41" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR41">41</a>] was used to retrieve the closest sequences to the bacteria associated with <em>Aplysina</em> species obtained in the present study. The resulting sequences were verified in terms of identity, host organism and sampling locations using the NCBI and comparing the information with those compiled by Simister et al. [<a href="#CR46" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR46">46</a>]. For further analysis, the following parameters were established for the removal of sequences that were recovered from marine sponges: (<strong><em>i</em></strong>) those with no information regarding host species or sampling sites and (<strong><em>ii</em></strong>) redundant, except when obtained from the same sponge species from distinct sampling sites.</p>
<p id="Par14">Preliminary phylogenetic inferences were performed in MEGA X [<a href="#CR47" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR47">47</a>, <a href="#CR48" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR48">48</a>] with the aim of removing redundant sequences. The resulting sequences from each phylum or class in the case of <em>Proteobacteria</em> were aligned using the MUSCLE software [<a href="#CR49" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR49">49</a>]. Phylogenetic inferences were performed with maximum likelihood method with a bootstrap of 100 random replicates. The best model for each tree was inferred within the MEGA software. For those preliminary trees the selected model was the Tamura-Nei model with a discrete gamma distribution of rate variation between locations (Γ4) and a proportion of invariant locations (I). The outgroups for all inferences were selected from Simister et al. [<a href="#CR46" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR46">46</a>] or sequences from our own database. Then, final phylogenetic inferences were generated using maximum likelihood method with a bootstrap of 1000 random replicates and the method selected by MEGA software was Jukes-Cantor [<a href="#CR50" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR50">50</a>] with uniform rates for all phyla and/or classes. Bootstrap values equal to or higher than 75% were considered to guarantee reliability of nodes [<a href="#CR51" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR51">51</a>, <a href="#CR52" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR52">52</a>].</p>
<p id="Par15">The phylogenetic inferences were also used to investigate whether or not the bacteria isolated from both <em>Aplysina</em> species belonged to sponge enriched clusters. These are defined as a clade containing at least three 16S rRNA gene sequences that are more similar to each other than to sequences from other sources and are derived from at least two different sponge species or the same species from distinct locations [<a href="#CR53" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR53">53</a>].</p></section><section id="Sec7"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Metabarcoding of the 16S rRNA gene sequencing and analyses</h2>
<p id="Par16">To verify the representativeness and novelty of the bacterial isolates obtained from <em>A. caissara</em> and <em>A. fulva</em>, a comparison was performed with the metabarcoding of both sponge species. To this end, 10 samples of <em>A. caissara</em> and five of <em>A. fulva</em> (Bioproject ID: PRJNA665805. <em>A. caissara</em>: SAMN16268819-SAMN16268823, PRJNA673577, <em>A. caissara</em>: SAMN16616778-SAMN16616782 and <em>A. fulva</em>: SAMN16616783- SAMN16616787), collected at Praia Preta (23°49’24.2”S 45°24’40.7”W) around 200 m away from Ponta Recife and Praia do Guaecá (23°49’13.44”S 45°28’8.4”W) were re-analyzed as explained by Nascimento-Silva et al. [<a href="#CR11" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR11">11</a>]. For more details regarding sampling, sponge identification and DNA extraction, see [<a href="#CR3" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR3">3</a>, <a href="#CR4" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR4">4</a>] Hardoim et al. [<a href="#CR3" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR3">3</a>, <a href="#CR4" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR4">4</a>].</p>
<p id="Par17">For the novelty, considering as putative new species those in which the sequence similarity was &lt; 98.65% and as new genera &lt; 95%, in this case, all the species within a new genus would be novel as well [<a href="#CR54" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR54">54</a>], all the amplicon sequence variants (ASVs) classified above these thresholds were removed from the dataset. To verify the ASVs that were exclusively obtained from each <em>Aplysina</em> species, Venn diagram analysis was carried out as explained above.</p>
<section id="Sec8"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Molecular screening of type I and II PKS and NRPS</h3>
<p id="Par18">The ketosynthase gene fragments for types I and II PKS and the adenylation domain gene fragments for NRPS were amplified as described in Table <a href="#Tab1" class="usa-link">1</a>. The master mixture for all three genes consisted of 1x PCR Master Mix (50 unit/ml of Taq DNA polymerase; 400 µM of each nucleotide and 3 mM of MgCl<sub>2</sub>, Promega), 10% of Dimethyl Sulfoxide (Sigma Aldrich), 1 µM of each primer and 1 µL (<em>c.</em> 50 ng/µL) of template DNA. The resulting amplicons were inspected in 1% agarose gel.
</p>

<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/Tab1/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div></section></section></section><section id="Sec9"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Antagonism assays</h2>
<p id="Par19">The capacity to produce antimicrobial substance (AMS) was evaluated in all isolated bacteria that were previously positive for at least one gene of interest in the molecular screen (type I and/or II PKS, and/or NRPS). The AMS test was performed as described by Marinho et al. [<a href="#CR56" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR56">56</a>] with some modifications. The bacterial strains were incubated in BHI and R2A broth for 48 h at 25°C. Afterwards, 10 µl of each bacteria culture was punctually inoculated on solid BHI and R2A media. Plates were incubated for 24 h at 25°C or until a visible colony was seen. Then, the colonies were exposed to chloroform vapor for 30 min to render them non-viable. Thus, any antimicrobial(s) produced by the bacteria would be already diffused on the agar plate and no more substance could be synthesized. The indicator bacterial strains <em>Staphylococcus aureus</em> (ATCC 29213, Gram-positive), <em>Pseudomonas aeruginosa</em> (ATCC 27853, Gram-positive), <em>Escherichia coli</em> (ATCC 25922, Gram-negative) and <em>Enterococcus faecium</em> (ATCC 19434, Gram-negative) were grown at 37°C in BHI prepared with deionized water. Then, the chloroform-treated colonies obtained from both <em>Aplysina</em> species were overlaid with BHI soft agar (37 g.L<sup>−1</sup> and 7.5 g.L<sup>−1</sup> agar prepared with deionized water) containing 30 µL of the overnight culture of the indicator bacterial strains. The plates were incubated for 24 h at 37°C and, after this period, the occurrence or not of inhibitory halos around the bacterial colonies from <em>Aplysina</em> species were verified. The bacteria were considered an AMS-positive only when the size of the halo was equal to or greater than 10 mm. These assays were performed in duplicate in both media.</p></section><section id="Sec10"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Extraction and sample preparation</h2>
<p id="Par20">Bacterial strains that tested positive in the molecular screening were cultivated at 25ºC in solid BHI and MB media. The stationary phase of the bacteria was verified approximately 72 h after plating. At this point, the cultures were macerated, eluted in 20 ml of ethyl acetate (Synth) and submitted to an ultrasonic bath (Nova Instruments), for 30 min. The liquid fraction was filtered and the residual agar was again dissolved and macerated in 10 ml of methanol (HPLC grade ≥ 99.9%, Sigma-Aldrich), filtered and combined to the previously obtained liquid fraction. Each sample was transferred to a rotary evaporator (Heidolph Instruments), placed in a water bath at 50ºC for solvent removal then transferred to a vacuum concentrator (Genevac miVac, Fisher Scientific) until completely dry, yielding the crude extracts.</p>
<p id="Par21">For LC-MS analysis, the crude extracts were subjected to a clean-up procedure using solid-phase extraction with C18 reverse phase cartridges (Chromabond, 45 μm, 500.0 mg, 6.0 mL). First, the C18 cartridges were pre-activated with 3.0 mL of methanol (MeOH) and equilibrated with 3 mL of MeOH/H<sub>2</sub>O (8:2, v/v). The dried extract (5.0 mg) was dissolved in 1.5 mL of MeOH/H<sub>2</sub>O (8:2, v/v), loaded onto a C18 cartridge, and sequentially eluted with 1.5 mL of the same solvent mixture. The dried eluates were reconstituted in MeOH to a concentration of 2.0 mg ml<sup>−1</sup>, centrifuged at 12,000 x <em>g</em> for 10 min, and the supernatants were collected into vials for subsequent analysis.</p>
<section id="Sec11"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Cytotoxic assay</h3>
<p id="Par22">The extracts were assessed for their cytotoxicity against the human colorectal cancer cells (HCT-116; ATCC CCR-247) through the MTT assay [<a href="#CR57" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR57">57</a>], as described by Tangerina et al. [<a href="#CR58" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR58">58</a>]. Briefly, cells in culture were harvested, diluted and seeded in 96-wells plates at 5 × 10<sup>4</sup> cells/mL. Cells were left to adhere for 24 h and the crude extracts, dissolved in DMSO, were then added to the cells at concentrations of 5 and 50 µg/mL. DMSO and doxorubicin made up negative and positive controls, respectively. After 72 h exposure, the wasted culture medium was removed and replaced in each well with fresh media added with 0.5 mg/mL of MTT to assess remaining metabolic activity of treated cells. Higher metabolic activity is revealed by greater reduction of MTT to a purple formazan precipitate, which was dissolved in 150 µL of DMSO and the absorbance measured using a plate spectrophotometer (Spectra Count Packard). Cytotoxicity of extracts was expressed as the percentage of inhibition of cell metabolic activity relative to that of the negative control.</p></section><section id="Sec12"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Mass spectrometry analysis</h3>
<p id="Par23">The capacity of the bacterial strains to produce bioactivity compounds was investigated. To this end, the ethyl acetate dried extracts were analyzed by liquid chromatography coupled with electrospray ionization high-resolution tandem mass spectrometry (LC-ESI-HRMS/MS). We used an ultra-fast liquid chromatography system (Prominence UFLC, Shimadzu) equipped with two solvent pumps (LC-20AD), degassing system (DGU-20A3), autosampler (SIL-20AHT), column oven (CTO-20 A), system controller (CBM-20 A) and a diode array detector (SPD-M20AV), connected to a micrOTOF-QII high resolution mass spectrometer (Bruker Daltonics). LC separations were performed on a 5 μm C18 (150 × 4.6 mm) Kinetex column (Phenomenex). A linear gradient with a mobile phase composed of water (solvent A) and acetonitrile (solvent B), both acidified with 0.1% formic acid from 5 to 100% (B) in 45 min was used for the elution of the samples. The injection volume was 10 ml.min<sup>−1</sup> and flow rate applied was 1 ml.min<sup>−1</sup>. Analytical blanks containing only the solvent MeOH were analyzed every 10 samples.</p>
<p id="Par24">The acquisition range for full-scan MS spectra was <em>m/z</em> 50–1500, in a data-dependent positive ion mode and parameters employed were as follows: nebulizer (N<sub>2</sub>) gas pressure of 4.5 bar, dry gas flow rate of 9 L.min<sup>−1</sup>, ion source temperature of 200 °C, capillary voltage of 4500 V, and voltage source of 5 kV. The fragmentation experiments were performed by collision-induced dissociation (CID) with the MS/MS fragmentation of five most intense selected ions and collision energy ramp of 30–60 eV. MS/MS active exclusion was set after four spectra and released after 30 s. The mass spectra were processed in the DataAnalysis software (version 4.3, Bruker Daltonics).</p></section><section id="Sec13"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Data processing and molecular networking (GNPS)</h3>
<p id="Par25">LC-MS/MS raw data files were converted to mzML format in the MSConvert (ProteoWizard) software using the filter Peak Picking with the vendor checked algorithm. The output data were uploaded directly to the Global Natural Products Social (GNPS, <a href="https://gnps.ucsd.edu" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">https://gnps.ucsd.edu</a><em>)</em> platform and a molecular network was created using the online workflow (<a href="https://ccms-ucsd.github.io/GNPSDocumentation/" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">https://ccms-ucsd.github.io/GNPSDocumentation/</a> [<a href="#CR59" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR59">59</a>]). The data was filtered by removing all MS/MS fragment ions within +/- 17 Da of the precursor <em>m/z</em>. MS/MS spectra were window filtered by choosing only the top six fragment ions in the +/- 50 Da window throughout the spectrum. The precursor ion mass tolerance was set to 0.02 Da and, MS/MS fragment ion tolerance of 0.05 Da, cosine score above 0.7 and more than four matched peaks. Finally, the maximum size of a molecular family was set to 100, and the lowest scoring edges were removed from molecular families until the molecular family size was below this threshold.</p>
<p id="Par26">The spectra in the network were then searched against GNPS’ spectral libraries. The library spectra were filtered in the same manner as the input data. All matches kept between network spectra and library spectra were required to have a score above 0.7 and at least four matched peaks. For those detected signals that did not have a match in the GNPS library, a manual spectral annotation was performed. The chemical formula was inferred from the exact mass, and the compounds were annotated based on comparisons with databases such as the Dictionary of Natural Products [<a href="#CR60" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR60">60</a>] and The Natural Products Atlas [<a href="#CR61" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR61">61</a>, <a href="#CR62" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR62">62</a>], as well as reported literature data. In addition, proposals were made by careful inspection based on the fragmentation patterns of each molecular family. The Insilico Peptidic Natural Products DEREPLICATOR bioinformatic tool was employed to annotate known peptidic natural products [<a href="#CR63" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR63">63</a>], using parameters such as precursor ion mass tolerance and MS/MS fragment ion tolerance of 0.02 and 0.05 Da, respectively, the VarQuest algorithm [<a href="#CR64" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR64">64</a>] and the PNP database selected, a maximum allowed charge of 2, a minimum of five amino acids (AA), a maximum allowed modification mass of 150 Da, and a minimum of five matched peaks with known compounds. Additionally, the molecular networks were subjected to MS2LDA (<a href="http://ms2lda.org/" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">http://ms2lda.org/</a> [<a href="#CR65" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR65">65</a>]), and Network Annotation Propagation (NAP [<a href="#CR66" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR66">66</a>] workflows. The GNPS MolNetEnhancer [<a href="#CR67" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR67">67</a>] workflow was employed to integrate data from the GNPS Library Search, MS2LDA, NAP, and Dereplicator into the molecular network, thus enhancing the chemical structural information within the network. ClassyFire [<a href="#CR68" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR68">68</a>] chemical ontology was used for the chemical class annotations. The molecular networks were represented visually using Cytoscape software v. 3.8.2 [<a href="#CR69" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR69">69</a>]. Detected features in both the solvent blank and media blank were removed from the resulting feature table. The results of these processing jobs can be accessed in Table S1.</p></section></section><section id="Sec14"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Results</h2>
<section id="Sec15"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Bacterial isolation and diversity</h3>
<p id="Par27">Due to the high densities of microbial cells in the mesohyls of <em>A. caissara</em> and <em>A. fulva</em> observed in transmission electron microscopy, both species were classified as HMA sponges (Fig. <a href="#Fig2" class="usa-link">2</a>a-b). A total of 209 bacterial isolates were obtained from the two sponge species investigated (Table <a href="#MOESM3" class="usa-link">S2</a>). From these, 60.3% were recovered from <em>A. fulva</em> and 39.7% from <em>A. caissara</em>. Overall, <em>Firmicutes</em> was the most abundant phyla associated with both species with 58% and <em>Proteobacteria</em> with 27.7% in <em>A. caissara</em> and 32.5% in <em>A. fulva</em> (Fig. <a href="#Fig3" class="usa-link">3</a>a). The main differences between the sponges were related to <em>Bacteroidota</em>, associated with 13.3% of the total in <em>A. caissara</em> and 3.2% in <em>A. fulva</em>; and <em>Actinobacteriota</em> with 1.2% in <em>A. caissara</em> and 6.3% in <em>A. fulva</em> (Fig. <a href="#Fig3" class="usa-link">3</a>a).
</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="Fig2"><h4 class="obj_head">Fig. 2.</h4>

<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/Fig3/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Bacterial phyla and the classes for the <em>Proteobacteria</em> associated with <em>Aplysina caissara</em> and <em>Aplysina fulva</em> obtained with (<strong>a</strong>) culture-dependent and (<strong>b</strong>) culture independent methods</p></figcaption></figure><p id="Par28">The 209 bacterial isolates were affiliated with 30 genera. From this total, 30% were exclusively associated with each <em>Aplysina</em> species, while 40% were common to both (Fig. <a href="#Fig4" class="usa-link">4</a>a). From <em>A. caissara</em> there was one representative of each genus (Table <a href="#MOESM4" class="usa-link">S3</a>a), while for <em>A. fulva</em>,<em> Ruegeria</em> and <em>Micrococcus</em> were the most abundant with 26.3% and 21%, respectively (Table <a href="#MOESM4" class="usa-link">S3</a>b). For the core, <em>Bacillus</em> (45.85%), <em>Staphylococcus</em> (9.94%), <em>Pseudovibrio</em> and <em>Vibrio</em> (8.84%, each) and <em>Fictibacillus</em> (8.29%) were the dominant genera (Table <a href="#MOESM4" class="usa-link">S3</a>c).</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="Fig4"><h4 class="obj_head">Fig. 4.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=11885781_42770_2024_1588_Fig4_HTML.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/06eb/11885781/3acab417d0b1/42770_2024_1588_Fig4_HTML.jpg" loading="lazy" id="MO4" height="251" width="750" alt="Fig. 4"></a></p>

<figcaption><p>Venn diagram containing all bacterial (<strong>a</strong>) strains isolated and (<strong>b</strong>) ASVs obtained from <em>Aplysina caissara</em> and <em>Aplysina fulva</em></p></figcaption></figure><p id="Par29">In this study, 42.6% of the bacteria obtained are putatively novel (89 strains, Table <a href="#MOESM3" class="usa-link">S2</a>). From this total, 84.3% were possible new species, and 15.7% could represent new genera. These were distributed as 46.7% of the strains isolated from <em>A. caissara</em> which could be new species and 57.1% new genera, while <em>A. fulva</em> showed 53.3% new species and 42.8% new genera (Table <a href="#MOESM3" class="usa-link">S2</a>).
</p></section><section id="Sec16"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Phylogenetic inferences</h3>
<p id="Par30">Phylogenetic inferences showed that bacterial strains belonging to all phyla associated with both <em>Aplysina</em> species encompassed sponge-enriched clusters. For <em>Actinobacteriota</em>, <em>Bacteroidota</em> and <em>Firmicutes</em>, 44.4%, 53.3% and 94.2%, respectively, of the bacterial strains belonged to clusters enriched in sponges (Figs. <a href="#MOESM1" class="usa-link">S1</a>-<a href="#MOESM1" class="usa-link">S3</a>). For <em>Alphaproteobacteria</em> and <em>Gammaproteobacteria</em>, these clusters comprised 75% and 58.3%, respectively, of the sequences recovered from both <em>Aplysina</em> species (Figs. <a href="#MOESM1" class="usa-link">S4</a>-<a href="#MOESM1" class="usa-link">S5</a>).</p></section><section id="Sec17"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Comparison of community structure</h3>
<p id="Par31">A total of 1,078.659 sequence reads for the V4-region of the 16 S rRNA gene were obtained after quality control, removal of chimera, singletons, mitochondria, chloroplast, ASVs classified as <em>Archaea</em> and above 98.65% and 95% threshold for novel species and genera, respectively, and unclassified at the domain-level, and they were assigned to 741 ASVs (Table <a href="#MOESM5" class="usa-link">S4</a>). Both sponge species were dominated by <em>Chloroflexi</em> (33.3% on average), <em>Gammaproteobacteria</em> (33.2%) and <em>Alphaproteobacteria</em> (7.46%), while <em>Actinobacteriota</em>, <em>Bacteroidota</em> and <em>Firmicutes</em> accounted, on average, for 3.5%, 1.9% and 0.6%, respectively, of the <em>Aplysina</em> species communities (Fig. <a href="#Fig3" class="usa-link">3</a>b, Table <a href="#MOESM6" class="usa-link">S5</a>).</p>
<p id="Par32">The 741 ASVs were assigned to 224 genera. From this total, 5.8% and 25.2% were solely detected in <em>A. caissara</em> and in <em>A. fulva</em>, respectively, while 69% were common to both species (Fig. <a href="#Fig4" class="usa-link">4</a>b). From <em>A. caissara</em> two genera were dominant: <em>Cronobacter</em> (ASV107, 33%) and UBA6055 (ASV162, 16.3%, Table <a href="#MOESM7" class="usa-link">S6</a>a), while for <em>A. fulva</em> Bin87 (ASV82, 11.23%) and <em>Pectobacterium</em> (ASV87, 11.21%, Table <a href="#MOESM7" class="usa-link">S6</a>b) were the predominant. For the core, <em>Cronobacter</em> (ASV1, 12.33%), Bin125 (ASV3, 3.6%) and Bin61 (ASV4, 3.6%) were the dominant genera (Table <a href="#MOESM7" class="usa-link">S6</a>c).</p>
<p id="Par33">Metabarcoding showed that 58.4% of the bacteria associated with both sponge species were putatively novel, representing 433 ASVs from a total of 741 ASVs (Table <a href="#MOESM5" class="usa-link">S4</a>). From these, 57 ASVs (13.2%) are possible new species and 376 (86.8%) new genera. They were distributed as 1.7% new species and 5.9% new genera among the ASVs associated with <em>A. caissara</em>, while <em>A. fulva</em> showed 21.1% as new species and 23.9% as new genera. Besides, 44 ASVs (77.2%) from a total of 57 detected in both <em>Aplysina</em> could be new species and 264 ASVs (70.2%) from a total of 376 might be new genera (Table <a href="#MOESM5" class="usa-link">S4</a>).</p></section><section id="Sec18"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Screening for types I and II PKS and NRPS genes</h3>
<p id="Par34">The molecular screening resulted in seven bacterial strains that amplified type I <em>PKS</em> genes, twelve for type II <em>PKS</em> and eight for <em>NRPS</em> (Table <a href="#Tab2" class="usa-link">2</a>). This indicated that 12.9% of the isolates from both <em>Aplysina</em> species can potentially produce molecules with biological activity. All isolates that amplified the type I <em>PKS</em> were affiliated with <em>Alphaproteobacteria</em>. Overall, from <em>A. caissara</em>, three bacterial strains were affiliated with <em>Pseudovibrio</em> and one with <em>Thalassospira</em>, while from <em>A. fulva</em>, two bacterial strains were affiliated with <em>Pseudovibrio</em> and one with <em>Labrenzia</em>, the latter being the only bacterium that is putatively a new species. In contrast, type II <em>PKS</em> was found in all taxa associated with both <em>Aplysina</em> species, except in <em>Bacteroidota</em> and <em>Gammaproteobacteria</em>. Among those with the gene, one <em>Micrococcus</em> can be considered a new species and two <em>Staphylococcus</em> could represent a new genus and new species. The <em>NRPS</em> gene was present in two strains from <em>A. caissara</em>, one of them representing a new genus and in six of <em>A. fulva</em>, with one new species.
</p>
<section class="tw xbox font-sm" id="Tab2"><h4 class="obj_head">Table 2.</h4>

Possible new species and genera are indicated with * and #, respectively</p></div>

<div class="tw-foot p"><div class="fn" id="_fn_p47"><p><sup>a</sup>Presence (+) ou absence (-) of PKS types I, II and NRPS target gene fragments</p></div></div></section></section><section id="Sec19"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Antimicrobial substance (AMS) production assays</h3>
<p id="Par35">Of the 22 bacterial strains that amplified at least one gene of interest, 12 showed biological activity in the AMS test, confirmed by the formation of an inhibition halo for at least one of the indicator bacteria (Table <a href="#Tab3" class="usa-link">3</a>). Six bacterial strains had positive results in the AMS test in each <em>A. caissara</em> (66,7%) and <em>A. fulva</em> (46,1%). This corresponds to 54.5% of the bacterial strains that contained type I and/or type II <em>PKS</em> and/or <em>NRPS</em> gene(s) potentially able to produce antimicrobial compounds.</p>

<div class="tw-foot p"><div class="fn" id="_fn_p51"><p>(-) = absence of inhibition halo</p></div></div></section><p id="Par36">Differences in the inhibition of the indicator bacteria were observed to be strongly related to the medium in which the sponge-associated strains were cultivated. <em>Pseudomonas aeruginosa</em> was only inhibited by bacteria grown in BHI while <em>Enterococcus faecium</em> was only affected by those from R2A medium. <em>Staphylococcus aureus</em> and <em>Escherichia coli</em> were inhibited by different bacterial strains grown in both media. However, none of the isolates, when grown on different media, showed activity against the same indicator bacteria.
</p></section><section id="Sec20"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Cytotoxicity assessment</h3>
<p id="Par37">Of the 22 bacterial isolates that were positive in the molecular screening, LIHM_A37, LIHM_A38, LIHM_45 and LIHM_105 did not grow on either media used. The extracts obtained from the remaining 18 isolates, cultured in MB and BHI media, were assayed for cytotoxicity against HCT-116 cells. Generally, the extracts did not reveal strong effects, with those obtained from bacteria cultured in MB media being slightly more effective than in BHI. The extract obtained from strain LIHM_564 (<em>Labrenzia</em>) from <em>A. fulva</em> and grown in MB showed the highest cytotoxicity overall (60.5%). This was followed by LIHM_551 (<em>Thalassospira</em>, 48.86%) and LIHM_31 (<em>Pseudovibrio</em>, 48.68%), both also from the same sponge species and culture media. Six other extracts showed cytotoxicity above 40%: in BHI LIHM_403 and LIHM_299 from <em>A. caissara</em>; in MB LIHM_294 and LIHM_543 from <em>A. caissara</em>, and LIHM_88 and LIHM_619 from <em>A. fulva</em> (Table <a href="#Tab4" class="usa-link">4</a>; Fig. <a href="#Fig5" class="usa-link">5</a>).
</p>

</div></div></section><figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="Fig5"><h4 class="obj_head">Fig. 5.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/06eb/11885781/e97e8dade7ac/42770_2024_1588_Fig5_HTML.jpg" loading="lazy" id="MO5" height="435" width="646" alt="Fig. 5"></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/Fig5/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Percentage inhibition of HCT-116 proliferation by bacterial strains grown on BHI (brain heart infusion) and MB (marine broth)</p></figcaption></figure></section><section id="Sec21"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Molecular networking and Metabolite annotation</h3>
<p id="Par38">Analysis of the bacterial extracts obtained from the two <em>Aplysina</em> species using the GNPS platform led to the generation of a comprehensive molecular network. This network featured 1843 precursor ions represented as distinct nodes and organized into 36 clusters based on their chemical similarity as measured by cosine scores (Fig. <a href="#Fig6" class="usa-link">6</a>). The significant number of nodes present in the molecular network suggests the considerable complexity of these bacterial samples.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="Fig6"><h4 class="obj_head">Fig. 6.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=11885781_42770_2024_1588_Fig6_HTML.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/06eb/11885781/b39a7ddb465a/42770_2024_1588_Fig6_HTML.jpg" loading="lazy" id="MO6" height="418" width="677" alt="Fig. 6"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/Fig6/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Molecular network derived from LC-MS/MS data of bacterial extracts in positive ESI mode. Panels (<strong>a</strong>-<strong>d</strong>) show detailed molecular network clusters corresponding to the annotated compounds: pumilacidins, glycerophospholipids, diketopiperazines and amino acids, respectively. Different node colors represent distinct bacterial genera, while the border color of each node denotes the type of annotation performed. Numbers within nodes correspond to exact m/z (Da) masses</p></figcaption></figure><p id="Par39">With regard to the culture media variable, the molecular network indicates that the BHI medium induced greater variation in the chemical profiles of the bacterial samples compared to marine medium (Fig. <a href="#MOESM1" class="usa-link">S6</a>). A number of compounds were noted only in cultures grown in BHI. Additionally, it was observed that certain clusters were composed solely by metabolites derived from bacteria grown in BHI; this indicates the unique properties of BHI that stimulate the production of these specific metabolites.
</p>
<p id="Par40">Using a set of tools available on the GNPS platform, it was possible to annotate a total of 14 compounds found in the bacterial extracts, including diketopiperazines, glycerophospholipids and cyclic lipopeptides. Table S7 provides a comprehensive annotation of the compounds in the bacterial extracts, including fragment ions and proposed molecular formulas. The putative annotations were confirmed at level 2 annotation using MS/MS library matching/reported literature and accurate mass measurement.</p>
<p id="Par41">The precursor ions at <em>m/z</em> 261.1250 [M + H]<sup>+</sup> (<strong>2</strong>), <em>m/z</em> 261.1228 [M + H]<sup>+</sup> (<strong>3</strong>), <em>m/z</em> 245.1294 [M + H]<sup>+</sup> (<strong>4</strong>), and <em>m/z</em> 245.1300 [M + H]<sup>+</sup> (<strong>5</strong>), attributed to the diketopiperazine class, were not grouped into the same cluster within the molecular network. These ions were specifically annotated due to the diagnostic ions produced by the cleavage of amino acid residues present in the cyclic dipeptides. For these compounds, the second-order fragmentation led to the formation of the main product ions at <em>m/z</em> 107 (<strong>2</strong> and <strong>3</strong>) and <em>m/z</em> 98 (<strong>4</strong> and <strong>5</strong>), which indicates the presence of the amino acid residues tyrosine and proline, respectively [<a href="#CR70" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR70">70</a>, <a href="#CR71" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR71">71</a>]. Additionally, the iminium ions at <em>m/z</em> 70 and <em>m/z</em> 120 are indicative of the proline and phenylalanine residues for compounds <strong>2</strong> and <strong>3</strong>, and <strong>4</strong> and <strong>5</strong>, respectively [<a href="#CR70" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR70">70</a>, <a href="#CR71" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR71">71</a>]. These metabolites were detected in all phyla, but only for bacterial extracts cultured in BHI.</p>
<p id="Par42">The second-order mass spectra for the precursor ions at <em>m/z</em> 452.2794 [M + H]<sup>+</sup> (<strong>6</strong>), <em>m/z</em> 454.2951 [M + H]<sup>+</sup> (<strong>7</strong>), <em>m/z</em> 480.3108 [M + H]<sup>+</sup> (<strong>8</strong>), and <em>m/z</em> 480.3123[M + H]<sup>+</sup> (<strong>9</strong>) displayed fragmentation patterns characteristic of glycerophospholipids with ethanolamine group substitution. These precursor ions were classified in the same cluster due to the diagnostic neutral loss of 141 Da, which corresponds to the phosphoethanolamine head group [<a href="#CR72" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR72">72</a>].</p>
<p id="Par43">The cyclic lipopeptides were found to be grouped within the same cluster in the molecular network. By analyzing the MS/MS mass spectra of the precursors ions at <em>m/z</em> 1036.6942 [M + H]<sup>+</sup> (<strong>10</strong>), <em>m/z</em> 1050.7115 [M + H]<sup>+</sup> (<strong>11</strong>), <em>m/z</em> 1064.7270 [M + H]<sup>+</sup> (<strong>12</strong>), <em>m/z</em> 1078.7437 [M + H]<sup>+</sup> (<strong>13</strong>), and <em>m/z</em> 1078.7378 [M + H]<sup>+</sup> (<strong>14</strong>), it was possible to identify distinct fragmentation patterns that align with those typical of the pumilacidin class. Compounds <strong>11</strong> to <strong>14</strong> exhibited characteristic fragmentations corresponding to the sequential neutral loss of the amino acid residues, 113-113-115-113-113-113 Da (from the Leu-Leu-Leu-Asp-Leu-Ile residues), varying only in the length of their fatty acid chains (C<sub>11</sub>H<sub>25</sub>-, C<sub>13</sub>H<sub>27</sub>-, C<sub>15</sub>H<sub>29</sub>-, and C<sub>15</sub>H<sub>29</sub>- respectively). Additionally, the main product ions of these compounds were found at <em>m/z</em> 699, attributed to the y<sub>6</sub> + H<sub>2</sub>O ion fragment, and that it was formed by losses of 351 Da (<strong>11</strong>), 365 Da (<strong>12</strong>) and 379 Da (<strong>13</strong> and <strong>14</strong>); and <em>m/z</em> 455, corresponding to the b<sub>3</sub> ion fragment [<a href="#CR73" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR73">73</a>–<a href="#CR75" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR75">75</a>]. Notably, compound <strong>10</strong> also shared similar MS/MS fragmentation patterns with other pumilacidin derivatives. However, its spectra display the fragment ion y<sub>6</sub> + H<sub>2</sub>O at <em>m/z</em> 685 [M + H-351]<sup>+</sup>; this indicates similar structure in the fatty acid chain to compound <strong>11</strong>. The observed mass difference of 14 Da between ions <em>m/z</em> 1050.7115 and 1036.6942 indicates that one of the amino acid units within these compounds likely possesses one less CH<sub>2</sub> group. In addition, a product ion sequence at <em>m/z</em> 923, 810, 695, 596, 483 and 370, with a mass difference of 113-113-115-99-113-113-113 Da, is supportive that this ion contains a valine unit instead of a leucine unit [<a href="#CR76" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR76">76</a>]. These compounds were found in samples from the genera <em>Bacillus</em> and <em>Pseudovibrio</em>.</p></section></section><section id="Sec22"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Discussion</h2>
<p id="Par44">Marine sponges are known for hosting abundant and complex microbial communities [<a href="#CR6" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR6">6</a>]. Seven <em>Aplysina</em> species were classified as HMA sponges based on transmission electron microscopy (TEM) analysis [<a href="#CR77" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR77">77</a>]. The Brazilian sponges <em>A. caissara</em> and <em>A. fulva</em> are classified for the first time as HMA sponges based on TEM analysis (Fig. <a href="#Fig2" class="usa-link">2</a>). Regarding the microbiome, between 18 and 25 bacterial phyla were found associated with <em>A. archeri</em>, <em>A. fulva</em>, <em>A. cavernicola</em>, <em>A. aerophoba</em> and <em>A. cauliformis</em> [<a href="#CR78" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR78">78</a>]. Recent studies showed that <em>A. caissara</em> and <em>A. fulva</em> individuals collected at the same or close locations as in the present study have between 35 and 39 bacterial phyla [<a href="#CR3" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR3">3</a>, <a href="#CR4" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR4">4</a>]. Those datasets were reanalyzed in the present study with a distinct approach and 21 bacterial phyla were found associated with both <em>Aplysina</em> species (Table <a href="#MOESM5" class="usa-link">S4</a>).</p>
<p id="Par45">Using culture-dependent approaches <em>Alphaproteobacteria</em> and <em>Gammaproteobacteria</em> were the dominant bacterial taxa associated with marine sponges, followed by <em>Actinobacteriota</em>, <em>Firmicutes</em> and <em>Bacteroidota</em> [<a href="#CR79" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR79">79</a>]. For several <em>Aplysina</em> species, the isolates were assigned to <em>Alphaproteobacteria</em>,<em> Gammaproteobacteria</em>, <em>Actinobacteriota</em> and <em>Firmicutes</em> [<a href="#CR24" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR24">24</a>], while the culturable assemblage of <em>A. aerophoba</em>, using three distinct cultivation techniques, was dominated by <em>Alphaproteobacteria</em>,<em> Firmicutes</em> and <em>Gammaproteobacteria</em> [<a href="#CR80" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR80">80</a>]. In this study, <em>Firmicutes</em> was the dominant phylum isolated from <em>A. caissara</em> and <em>A. fulva</em>, followed by <em>Alphaproteobacteria</em>, <em>Gammaproteobacteria</em>, <em>Bacteroidota</em> and <em>Actinobacteriota</em> (Fig. <a href="#Fig3" class="usa-link">3</a>a). This contrasts with other culture-dependent study that showed that <em>Firmicutes</em> was only a minor component of the microbiome of <em>Aplysina</em> [<a href="#CR81" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR81">81</a>] and other sponges [<a href="#CR16" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR16">16</a>, <a href="#CR82" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR82">82</a>]. Similar result was also obtained with cultivation-independent studies [<a href="#CR6" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR6">6</a>], including <em>A. caissara</em> and <em>A. fulva</em> collected at the same or nearby sampling sites that were reanalyzed in the present study (Fig. <a href="#Fig3" class="usa-link">3</a>b). Even with these discrepancies, over 94% of the <em>Firmicutes</em> strains isolated from both <em>Aplysina</em> as well as 44.4% of the <em>Actinobacteriota</em> and 75% of <em>Gammaproteobacteria</em> belonged to sponge enriched clusters (Figs. <a href="#MOESM1" class="usa-link">S1</a>, <a href="#MOESM1" class="usa-link">S3</a>, <a href="#MOESM1" class="usa-link">S5</a>), revealing close interactions between the host sponge and its bacteriome.</p>
<p id="Par46">The culturable assemblage obtained from both <em>Aplysina</em> species in this work revealed that 15.7% were putatively new bacterial genera and 84.3% new species, demonstrating the great diversity and novelty in cultivable bacterial symbionts (Table <a href="#MOESM3" class="usa-link">S2</a>). This contrasts with what was found in the same species using the culture-independent approach. In this case, a much larger proportion could be considered as new genera: 86.8% of the ASVs, while 13.2% could be new species. Together, these results reinforce the still incipient knowledge about the microbial diversity of the sponge holobiont, which are often based on estimates or theoretical models [<a href="#CR83" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR83">83</a>]. Also, even with the well-known biases involved with traditional agar plate-based cultivation [<a href="#CR84" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR84">84</a>–<a href="#CR89" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR89">89</a>], this approach was capable to generate 42.6% of novelty within the whole dataset, which can be further investigated regarding their putative function for the sponge host and biotechnological potential [<a href="#CR20" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR20">20</a>].</p>
<p id="Par47">Notably, <em>Firmicutes</em>, <em>Alphaproteobacteria</em> and <em>Gammaproteobacteria</em> appear in similar percentages in both sponges, which indicated the abundance of cultivable bacteria of these taxa in both <em>Aplysina</em> species (Fig. <a href="#Fig2" class="usa-link">2</a>). In fact, 64.1% of a total of 181 strains affiliated with <em>Firmicutes</em>, 17.1% with <em>Gammaproteobacteria</em>, 11% with <em>Alphaproteobacteria</em> and 8% with <em>Bacteroidota</em> were detected in both bacteriomes (Table <a href="#MOESM3" class="usa-link">S2</a>c). However, there were five <em>Firmicutes</em> strains, one <em>Alphaproteobacteria</em> and one <em>Gammaproteobacteria</em>, in addition to one <em>Actinobacteriota</em> and one <em>Bacteroidota</em> that were exclusively detected in <em>A. caissara</em> (Table <a href="#MOESM3" class="usa-link">S2</a>a). The exclusive bacteriome associated with <em>A. fulva</em> belonged to <em>Actinobacteriota</em> (eight strains, Table <a href="#MOESM3" class="usa-link">S2</a>c), <em>Alphaproteobacteria</em> (eight strains) and <em>Gammaproteobacteria</em> (three strains). <em>Firmicutes</em> was not solely found associated with <em>A. fulva</em>. Overall, this indicates that <em>A. caissara</em> and <em>A. fulva</em> potentially host bacteriomes that can be distinguished by slightly different enriched portions of their members even when culture-dependent approaches are used.</p>
<p id="Par48"><em>Actinobacteriota</em>, <em>Alphaproteobacteria</em> and <em>Firmicutes</em> associated with sponge species are generally characterized by their biological activities, potentially contributing to the protection of the host and other organisms. As such, the biotechnological potential of these taxa is highly attractive for the bioprospecting field [<a href="#CR24" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR24">24</a>, <a href="#CR90" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR90">90</a>, <a href="#CR91" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR91">91</a>]. It also holds true in the present study, where a total of 10.5% bacterial isolates showed positive results for the presence of type I and/or type II <em>PKS</em> and/or <em>NRPS</em>, indicating new potential targets for bioprospecting. These genes could be an important part of the host’s physiology due to their capabilities to produce biosurfactants and several antimicrobial agents [<a href="#CR39" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR39">39</a>, <a href="#CR92" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR92">92</a>]. Seven bacterial strains were positive for the presence of the type I <em>PKS</em> gene and they were mainly affiliated with <em>Alphaproteobacteria</em>, with a predominance of the genus <em>Pseudovibrio</em>. Screenings of the same gene in bacteria isolated from other marine sponges collected in different locations, showed similar results, with this gene also predominating in <em>Pseudovibrio</em> communities [<a href="#CR16" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR16">16</a>, <a href="#CR93" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR93">93</a>, <a href="#CR94" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR94">94</a>]. Analysis of some <em>Pseudovibrio</em> strains obtained from marine sponges showed the presence of several biosynthetic gene clusters [<a href="#CR95" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR95">95</a>]. The type II <em>PKS</em> gene was the most representative when compared to the others, with 5.7% of bacterial strains distributed in seven genera, with the vast majority assigned to <em>Alphaproteobacteria</em>, known for possessing <em>PKS</em> systems [<a href="#CR93" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR93">93</a>, <a href="#CR94" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR94">94</a>]. However, other screenings of type II <em>PKS</em> gene in bacteria from marine sediments and different sponges did not detect its presence [<a href="#CR92" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR92">92</a>, <a href="#CR96" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR96">96</a>] or it was present in very low numbers in metagenomic study [<a href="#CR97" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR97">97</a>]. As such, it should be noted that there are still no studies that have obtained a positive result in the screening of type II <em>PKS</em> in <em>Alphaproteobacteria</em> isolated from marine sponges. For the <em>NRPS</em> gene, amplification was observed in 2.9% of the isolates of <em>Alphaproteobacteria</em> and in 0.95% of <em>Firmicutes</em>. There is evidence showing the existence of <em>NRPS</em> clusters in both phyla, tracked in strains associated with different sponge species collected in China and Portugal, as well as in other hosts and/or environments [<a href="#CR98" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR98">98</a>–<a href="#CR103" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR103">103</a>]. <em>Proteobacteria</em> and <em>Firmicutes</em> are commonly observed as having the greatest amplifications of the types I and II <em>PKS</em> and <em>NRPS</em> genes. These results also reveal nuances about how metabolic activities and production of natural compounds are important both for bioprospecting and for ecological studies of the interactions between bacteria and marine sponges [<a href="#CR32" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR32">32</a>, <a href="#CR36" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR36">36</a>, <a href="#CR93" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR93">93</a>, <a href="#CR104" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR104">104</a>–<a href="#CR106" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR106">106</a>].</p>
<p id="Par49">Antimicrobial activities against different pathogenic strains were exhibited by species of the genera <em>Vibrio</em>, <em>Bacillus</em>, <em>Pseudovibrio</em>, <em>Staphylococcus</em>, <em>Ruegeria</em> and <em>Streptomyces</em>, obtained from distinct locations and sponge species [<a href="#CR24" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR24">24</a>, <a href="#CR90" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR90">90</a>, <a href="#CR91" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR91">91</a>, <a href="#CR107" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR107">107</a>]. The biological activity of the genus <em>Micrococcus</em> was the most prominent of the phylum <em>Actinobacteriota</em>, corroborating data observed in previous research with bacterial strains isolated from marine sponges [<a href="#CR91" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR91">91</a>, <a href="#CR108" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR108">108</a>]. Literature on cytotoxicity of bacterial isolates defines results of 50 to 75% inhibition of tumor cell lines as having moderate cytotoxic activity [<a href="#CR109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR109">109</a>, <a href="#CR110" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR110">110</a>]. In the present work, an <em>Alphaproteobacteria</em> (LIHM_564) produced the most cytotoxic extract among the 18 isolates, which showed moderate cytotoxic activity, reducing over 50% of cell growth. The isolate, detected exclusively associated with <em>A. fulva</em>, was identified as <em>Labrenzia</em>, but with an identity score that indicated new species (95.9%). This genus is recognized for its bioprospecting potential due to the presence of biosynthetic genes fundamental in the production of polyketides, peptides and terpenes [<a href="#CR111" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR111">111</a>]. Previous works with isolates of this genus from different sources showed its cytotoxicity against a variety of different tumor cell lines [<a href="#CR112" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR112">112</a>–<a href="#CR114" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR114">114</a>]. In this context, it is also worth mentioning the extracts obtained herein from <em>Pseudovibrio</em> and <em>Thalassospira</em>, which inhibited at least 50% cell growth in HCT-116. These are well known genera of bacteria occurring in marine environments and sources of natural products reported for various bioactivities [<a href="#CR115" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR115">115</a>–<a href="#CR118" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR118">118</a>].</p>
<p id="Par50">Under controlled growth conditions, the influence of the culture media on their metabolic production becomes highly pronounced (Fig. <a href="#MOESM1" class="usa-link">S7</a>). The specialized metabolites produced by the bacteria can significantly vary with the type and composition of the culture media used. As observed through the molecular network analysis, bacteria cultivated in BHI medium demonstrated greater metabolic diversity compared to those grown in marine medium. This greater diversity is probably due to the nutrient-rich environment provided by BHI, which optimally facilitates the growth and metabolic processes of a wide range of bacterial species. This points out to the influential role that the culture media plays in shaping the bacterial metabolic production. However, when comparing the influence of each host sponge species on the metabolic diversity, it was observed that they were similar, despite differences in the associated microbiota. This is in agreement with the observation that the microbial community of both sponge species is assembled by deterministic processes [<a href="#CR3" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR3">3</a>], viz. directed by hosts traits, interspecies interactions and environmental factors such as nutrients, temperature and salinity shape the assembly of microbial communities [<a href="#CR119" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR119">119</a>, <a href="#CR120" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR120">120</a>]. Besides, the functional redundancy [<a href="#CR6" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR6">6</a>] would allow distinct bacteria present in the bacteriome of each <em>Aplysina</em> species to produce similar metabolic diversity.</p>
<p id="Par51">The molecular network representation highlights the metabolic diversity across samples, illustrating the complexities inherent in the relationship between bacterial species, their specific growth conditions and their associated sponge hosts (Fig. <a href="#Fig6" class="usa-link">6</a> and <a href="#MOESM1" class="usa-link">S6</a>). The representation sheds light on the intricate interplay of these factors, demonstrating their key role in shaping the diverse metabolic profiles observed in these bacteria samples. A substantial number of compounds remained unannotated, thus implying the presence of potential, yet uncharacterized metabolites within these extracts. Due to the limited annotation of individual compounds, MolNetEnhancer, a molecular class-based annotation approach, was employed. Using this approach, it was possible to observe a predominance of groups associated with prenol lipids, fatty acids and peptidomimetics (Fig. <a href="#MOESM1" class="usa-link">S7</a>), thereby offering valuable insight into the extensive metabolic profile of these bacterial samples. In marine sponges, peptides/peptidomimetics have been described to exhibit antibacterial, antifungal, antiviral, anti-inflammatory and cytotoxic activities [<a href="#CR121" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR121">121</a>]. However, we cannot rule out that some of the compounds are indeed produced by associated bacteria. Five compounds were classified as pumilacidins, which are variants of surfactins reported to have numerous biological activities such as antifungal, antibacterial, anti-inflammatory and antiparasitic. These compounds are synthesized via NRPS and produced by several <em>Bacillus</em> species, including strains isolated from the Mediterranean <em>A. aerophoba</em> [<a href="#CR74" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR74">74</a>, <a href="#CR75" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR75">75</a>, <a href="#CR122" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR122">122</a>–<a href="#CR124" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR124">124</a>]. Additionally, pumilacidins were also obtained from a mangrove species of <em>Bacillus</em> and showed results for the bioremediation of petroleum and its byproducts [<a href="#CR125" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR125">125</a>]. To note here is that both sponge species in our work were collected from a site polluted with polycyclic aromatic hydrocarbons [<a href="#CR3" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR3">3</a>, <a href="#CR4" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR4">4</a>, <a href="#CR10" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR10">10</a>]. Five compounds were identified as diketopiperazines and they are catalyzed by NRPSs (&gt; 100 kDa) and cyclodipeptide synthases (30 kDa) [<a href="#CR126" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR126">126</a>]. Those compounds also exhibited a variety of activities such as antimicrobials and antitumors detected in <em>Bacillus</em>, <em>Streptomyces</em> and an unidentified bacterial strain associated with other sponge species [<a href="#CR126" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR126">126</a>]. Overall, these compounds could be assisting the <em>Aplysina</em> species to survive and thrive in a polluted area. The number of uncharacterized metabolites calls for more research in an attempt to characterize and investigate them in depth and also the biotechnological potential of the bacteria isolated from marine sponges.</p>
<p id="Par52">Of note, the bacterial abundance and diversity detected in this work refers only to cultivable strains. However, this usually represents just 0.1 to 11% of the total bacteria associated with sponges [<a href="#CR127" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR127">127</a>–<a href="#CR131" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR131">131</a>]. This demonstrates the difficulties found when trying to replicate the ideal conditions for the growth of these microorganisms in vitro, which is a major challenge faced in several scientific fields, mainly in bioprospecting [<a href="#CR132" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR132">132</a>].</p>
<p id="Par53">In this study we showed that the culturable assemblage associated with <em>A. caissara</em> and <em>A. fulva</em> were distinct. The vast majority of strains formed sponge-enriched clusters confirming their true association with these animals. Some strains were exclusively detected in each <em>Aplysina</em> species, showing that the bacteriome could be used to differentiate between the two hosts, even when a culture-dependent approach was used. Additionally, there was a large number of putative new bacterial species and genus detected in this study. In the molecular screening, several strains showed the presence of type I and/or type II <em>PKS</em> and/or <em>NRPS</em> genes, with further tests demonstrating their antitumor and antimicrobial capabilities. From these selected strains, numerous compounds were detected, such as diketopiperazine, glycerophospholipids and pumilacidins. However, the number of unidentified compounds in each strain was extremely high, which calls for further investigation not only on their structure and potentials but also their putative function for the host sponge.</p></section><section id="Sec25"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Supplementary Information</h2>

<p> All 16S rRNA gene sequences were deposited in the National Center for Biotechnology Information, under accession numbers from <a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/nuccore/?term=MZ503279:MZ503487[pacc]" class="usa-link" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">MZ503279 to MZ503487</a>. All 16S rRNA metabarcoding datasets used in this study were deposited as Sequence Read Archive in NCBI database with Bioproject IDs: PRJNA665805 (SAMN16268819-SAMN16268823) and PRJNA673577 (SAMN16616778-SAMN16616782 and SAMN16616783- SAMN16616787). The mass spectrometry data collected from bacterial extracts can be publicly accessed on the Mass spectrometry Interactive Virtual Environment (MassIVE) under the dataset number MSV000092343.</p></section></section></section><footer class="p courtesy-note font-secondary font-sm text-center"><hr class="headless">
<p>Articles from Brazilian Journal of Microbiology are provided here courtesy of <strong>Brazilian Society of Microbiology</strong></p></footer></section></article>
